Example 10
(Fourth order quintic nonlinear Schrödinger equation:
n=4
)

In this case we know
\mathfrak{e}_{4}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
.
For this and higher orders, our procedure needs to be systematic. The Pöppe polynomial in this
case has the form,

\displaystyle\pi_{4}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;c_{4}\cdot[\boldsymbol{4}]+c_{200}\cdot[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}+c_{020}[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+c_{002}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}\,[\boldsymbol{2}]

\displaystyle\;+c_{110}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]^{2}\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+c_{101}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]+c_{011}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]^{2}+c_{00000}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]^{5}.

The signature expansion for
[\boldsymbol{4}]
has the form,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{4}]=

\displaystyle\;\chi(4)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(31)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(22)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(13)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(211)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(121)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(112)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1111)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}].

Using the skew and symmetric Pöppe products in Lemma
8
we find, for example, that,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\chi(2)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(11)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)\,\bigl(\chi(0\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\chi(2{{\otimes}}0\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}3\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}\{2\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]\bigr)+\chi(2\hat{{{\otimes}}}0\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(11{{\otimes}}0\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\{2\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]\bigr)

\displaystyle\;+\chi(11\hat{{{\otimes}}}0\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}].

The other products shown in
\pi_{4}
can be similarly expanded. In Table
2
we list all the basis elements
and corresponding coefficients generated by all the Pöppe products present in
\pi_{4}
. The values of the coefficients
are the
\chi
-images of the tensored terms shown. Each row generates a linear algebraic
equation for the expansion coefficients
c_{4}
,
c_{200}
,
c_{110}
, …
c_{00000}
. Note that in Table
2

rows are ordered according to descent order, with a sub-order for the positions of the
\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}
letters
as indicated. The ordering of the columns is self-evident from the structure present in the table. We discuss
this ordering in more explicitly in Section
6
.
Using all the rows shown, we generate an over-determined linear system of algebraic equations,
AC=B
,
where
B
is the column vector shown in the right-hand column in Table
2
,
C
is the vector of coefficients
c_{4}
,

c_{020}
, and so forth in the order shown across the top row. The matrix
A
consists of the
\chi
-images of the entries shown in the table
(neglecting the final column).
From the augmented matrix
[A\,B]
, we observe the first two rows generate a closed system of equations,
namely
c_{4}+2c_{020}=0
and
-2c_{020}=1
. This system of equations corresponds to the following smaller
augmented matrix subsystem
[A_{0}\,B_{0}]
for
c_{4}
and
c_{020}
, where

A_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&-2\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

Hence we deduce
c_{4}=1
and
c_{020}=-\frac{1}{2}
. With these values in hand, we then observe that the next two
rows also generate a closed system of equations for
c_{200}
and
c_{011}
given by
4c_{4}+2c_{020}+2c_{200}+2c_{011}=0
and
2c_{020}-2c_{200}+2c_{011}=0
.
This linear system of equations for the two unknowns
c_{200}
and
c_{011}
is represented by the smaller augmented matrix subsystem
[A_{1}\,B_{1}]
, where,

A_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}2&2\\
-2&2\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-3\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

Solving the linear system of equations, we deduce that
c_{200}=-1
and
c_{011}=-1/2
. The next four rows in the augmented matrix
[A\,B]
,
given the coefficients we have already solved for, generate a closed system of equations for
c_{110}
,
c_{101}
,
c_{002}
and
c_{00000}
,
generated by the smaller augment matrix
[A_{2}\,B_{2}]
, where,

A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&2&4\\
-1&1&0&-4\\
1&-1&0&-4\\
-1&-1&2&4\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-5/2\\
-5/2\\
-1/2\\
-3/2\end{pmatrix}.

The solution to this system is given by
c_{110}=-1/2
,
c_{101}=-3/2
,
c_{002}=-1
and
c_{00000}=3/8
.
It is easy to check the equations represented by the remaining rows in the big augmented matrix
[A\,B]

above, are consistent. Thus, we deduce that
\mathfrak{e}_{4}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\pi_{4}
, where the coefficients
c_{4}
,
c_{020}
and so forth,
are given by the unique values outlined above.
The fourth order non-commutative nonlinear Schrödinger equation for
g=[\![G]\!]
, with
\mathcal{M}_{4}=\mu_{4}\mathrm{i}\mathcal{I}
, is given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{-1}{\partial}_{t}g=

\displaystyle\;{\partial}^{4}g-({\partial}^{2}g)g^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}g({\partial}^{2}g)g-g^{2}({\partial}^{2}g)

\displaystyle\;-\tfrac{1}{2}({\partial}g)^{2}g-\tfrac{3}{2}({\partial}g)g({\partial}g)-\tfrac{1}{2}g({\partial}g)^{2}+\tfrac{3}{8}g^{5}.

This matches the form given in Malham
Malham:quinticNLS
and Nijhoff et al.
(
NQVDLCI
, eq. B.4a)
.

Remark 14
(Basis elements)

In Tables
1
and
2
we record the basis elements of the form

[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
in the far left column. The composition components
w
are compositions of
n
,
i.e. compositions of
3
and
4
, in the respective tables. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
-component
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in the basis element
is in principle any possible
(|w|+1)
-tuples that can be constructed from

\{\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. However, recall
\bigl[(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{\dagger}\bigr]=-[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]

and the Definition
8
.
Using this property for
\bigl[(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{\dagger}\bigr]
, for any basis element
[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
, we can thus always
arrange for the first component of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
to be ‘
\boldsymbol{0}
’; as can be observed in the tables.
The basis elements are ordered according to descent order for the compositions
w
and natural binary ordering for the

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
-components
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
. For more details see Definition
15

in Section
6
, and the subsequent discussion therein.
Note that though the first component of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
can always be arranged to be
\boldsymbol{0}
,
in our computations involving Pöppe products, we often utilise the symmetry
\bigl[(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})^{\dagger}\bigr]=-[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]

in order to use the Pöppe products listed in Lemma
8
.
Thus temporarily, the first component in some factors is sometimes
\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}
.
However, we always use the same symmetry again to convert the final answer to the form with
\boldsymbol{0}
as the
first component in the basis element.

Non-zero signature coefficients appearing
in the expansion of the
Pöppe polynomial

\pi_{4}
in Example
10
.

\chi
-images of the signature entries shown.

shown in the very left column, for each of the monomials in
\pi_{4}
shown
across the top row. The final column represents the coefficient on the right-hand side of the equation
\pi_{4}=[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
.

-4\cdot(0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0)

[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

0{{\otimes}}11{{\otimes}}0

0{{\otimes}}1{{\otimes}}1

-1{{\otimes}}1{{\otimes}}0

-1{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}1

2\cdot(0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}2)

[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}]

13

0\hat{{{\otimes}}}2{{\otimes}}0

2\cdot(1{{\otimes}}1{{\otimes}}0)

[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

-0\hat{{{\otimes}}}2{{\otimes}}0

[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]

211

121

16\cdot(0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}0)

5
Non-commutative Lax hierarchy and the sine-Gordon equation

Herein we establish the non-commutative nonlinear Schrödinger and modified Korteweg–de Vries Lax hierarchy iteratively,
order by order. The non-commutative modified Korteweg–de Vries hierarchy can be found for example in Carillo and Schiebold
(
CSI
, eq. (9))
.
Importantly, this iterative hierarchy extends to all negative orders. The first member of negative order, i.e. for which
n=-1
, corresponds to
the non-commutative sine-Gordon cubic-form equation in Example
2
; see, for example, Tracy and Widom
TW
for the scalar case.
Establishing the hierarchy for all orders
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is particularly simple in the Pöppe algebra
\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}
.
We need to define some natural actions on
\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}
first.

Definition 14
(Adjoint and symmetric algebra products and actions)

We define the standard commutation and symmetric products, respectively,

\mathrm{ad}\colon\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}\times\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}\to\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}
and

\mathrm{sd}\colon\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}\times\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}\to\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}
.
For example, for
[\boldsymbol{0}]\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]\subset\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}

and any word
w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{C}{\langle}\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}{\rangle}
we have,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle\coloneqq[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})-(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,[\boldsymbol{0}],

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})

\displaystyle\coloneqq[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})+(w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi})\,[\boldsymbol{0}],

which is the exclusive form of their action we use below.
We also define the following two actions on the skew-Pöppe algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
.
For
[\boldsymbol{0}]\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
set,

\displaystyle A\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{ad}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}{\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\mathrm{ad}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]},

\displaystyle S\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}{\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}.

That the actions of
A
and
S
are closed in
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
is established as part of
the proof of the following crucial lemma.

Lemma 10
(Natural iteration)

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have:

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{d}}-A)[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}],

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{d}}-S)[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}].

Proof

By direct computation using Lemma
8
we have,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{0}][\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]-[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}][\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=2\cdot\bigl(\{\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}1^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\cdot{\mathfrak{d}}\,\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}.

Hence we observe, using that
\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}=-\{\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}n\boldsymbol{0}\}
we have,

\displaystyle A\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}n\boldsymbol{0}\}\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]-[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}]+[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]+[\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}n\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle={\mathfrak{d}}\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]-[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}].

This gives the first result. Again, by direct computation, we have,

\displaystyle\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]=

\displaystyle\;[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]+[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2\,\bigl(\{\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2\cdot{\mathfrak{d}}\,\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}\}.

Hence we observe,

\displaystyle S\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]=

\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}\}+\{\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}\}\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}]+[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]+[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]-[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;{\mathfrak{d}}\,[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]-[\boldsymbol{0}(n+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}].

This gives the second result.∎

The following immediate corollary is established straightforwardly by induction, both when
n
is positive as well as negative.
For the remainder of this section we refer to Pöppe polyomials
\pi_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, though in Definition
13
,

n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
for which
\pi_{n}=\pi_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}],[\boldsymbol{1}],\ldots,[\boldsymbol{n}]\bigr)
. The form of
\pi_{n}
for the
negative integer cases is given presently.

Corollary 1
(Non-commutative Lax hierarchy iteration)

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
be a given integer, and consider the equation,
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\pi_{n}
,
where
\pi_{n}
is a Pöppe polynomial and
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)
equals
[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]
if
n
is odd, and equals
[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
if
n
is even.
For
n=0,1,2,3,4
such polynomials
\pi_{n}
exist, as demonstrated in Examples
6
—
10
. Then we have,

\mathfrak{e}_{n+1}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\begin{cases}({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\pi_{n},&\text{when $n$ is even,}\\
({\mathfrak{d}}-S)\pi_{n},&\text{when $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}

Proof

This follows directly from Lemma
10
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by induction.

Further, we have the following additional immediate corollary.

Corollary 2
(Non-commutative Lax hierarchy)

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
(n+1)
th order member equation of the non-commutative Lax hierarchy is given by,

\mathfrak{e}_{n+1}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\begin{cases}({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\,\bigl(({\mathfrak{d}}-S)({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\bigr)^{\frac{n}{2}}\,[\boldsymbol{0}],&\text{when $n$ is even,}\\
\bigl(({\mathfrak{d}}-S)({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}(n+1)}\,[\boldsymbol{0}],&\text{when $n$ is odd.}\end{cases}

The Lax hierarchy stated in Corollary
2
, at each odd order
n
, exactly matches that quoted
for the non-commutative modified Korteweg–de Vries hierarchy in Carillo and Schiebold
(
CSI
, eq. (9))
.
Given the main existence and uniqueness result we prove in Section
6
, this is expected.
The cases
n=0,1,2,3,4
in Corollary
2
naturally match the non-commutative equation
members given in Examples
6
—
10
. However, Corollary
2
also
applies for negative
n
. Consider the example case of order ‘
-1
’.

Example 11
(Non-commutative sine-Gordon cubic-form equation: order ‘
-1
’)

Setting
n=-2
, a case when
n
is even, in Corollary
2
, generates the equation,

\mathfrak{e}_{-1}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\bigl(({\mathfrak{d}}-S)({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\bigr)^{-1}\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad({\mathfrak{d}}-S)\,\mathfrak{e}_{-1}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}].

We can express the equation on the right as follows,

{\mathfrak{d}}\mathfrak{e}_{-1}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}]+\tfrac{1}{4}\Bigl(\bigl({\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{-1}([\boldsymbol{0}]^{2})\bigr)\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl({\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\mathfrak{e}_{-1}([\boldsymbol{0}]^{2})\bigr)\Bigr),

where we have used that
\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}\,\mathfrak{e}_{-1}([\boldsymbol{0}])=\mathfrak{e}_{-1}([\boldsymbol{0}]^{2})
from Lemma
11
just below.
This relation in
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
translates to the non-commutative sine-Gordon cubic-form equation
given in Example
2
for
g=[\![G]\!]
, with
\mathcal{M}_{-1}=\mu_{-1}\,{\mathrm{id}}
, i.e.

{\partial}{\partial}_{t}g-\tfrac{1}{4}\bigl(({\partial}^{-1}{\partial}_{t}g^{2})\,g+g\,({\partial}^{-1}{\partial}_{t}g^{2})\bigr)=\mu_{-1}g.

Invoking the factor ‘2’ rescaling mentioned in Remark
13
gives the exact match.

Lemma 11

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have,

\mathfrak{e}_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}\bigr)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{sd}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}\,\mathfrak{e}_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr),&\text{when $n$ is odd},\\
\mathrm{ad}_{[\boldsymbol{0}]}\,\mathfrak{e}_{n}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr),&\text{when $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof

This result is established at the operator level. Recall
\mathcal{M}_{n}\coloneqq-\mu_{n}(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{I})^{n-1}

and the properties outlined in Lemma
9
. We observe when
n
is even, we have,

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}[V]^{2}=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{M}_{n}\bigl[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V^{\dagger}\bigr]\,[V]+[V]\,\mathcal{M}_{n}\bigl[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V^{\dagger}\bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{M}_{n}\bigl[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V^{\dagger}\bigr]\,[V]-\mathcal{M}_{n}[V]\,\bigl[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V^{\dagger}\bigr],

where we have used that
[V]\,\mathcal{M}_{n}=[\![V-V^{\dagger}]\!]\,\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{M}_{n}\,[\![V^{\dagger}-V]\!]=-\mathcal{M}_{n}[V]
.
When
n
is odd, we follow an analogous computation with
[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V]
replacing
[V(\mathrm{i}P)_{n}V^{\dagger}]
just above,
and that in this case there is no sign change in the second term on the right as

[V]\,\mathcal{M}_{n}=[\![V-V^{\dagger}]\!]\,\mathcal{M}_{n}=\mathcal{M}_{n}\,[\![V-V^{\dagger}]\!]=\mathcal{M}_{n}[V]
.
∎

Naturally we can continue to consider further negative order hierarchy member equations. For example,
the non-commutative order ‘
-2
’ equation can be generated by setting
n=-3
, a case when
n
is odd,
in Corollary
2
. With
\mathfrak{e}_{-2}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}(-2)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
this generates the non-commutative equation,

\mathfrak{e}_{-2}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\bigl(({\mathfrak{d}}-S)({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\bigr)^{-1}\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad({\mathfrak{d}}-S)({\mathfrak{d}}-A)\,\mathfrak{e}_{-2}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}].

And so forth.
The example integrable equations in Examples
6
–
10
in Section
4

were of the form
{\partial}_{t}g=\pi(g,{\partial}g,{\partial}^{2}g,\ldots)
for
n=0,1,2,3,4
. Indeed for these examples, we show
the equation is unique in this class. In other words, at each of the orders considered, given the base dispersion equation
for
P
and the form of the Marchenko equation, the right-hand side in the non-commutative nonlinear partial differential equation,
the ‘nonlinear field’, is of the form
\pi=\pi(g,{\partial}g,{\partial}^{2}g,\ldots)
, where
\pi
is a polynomial in its arguments.
In Section
6
, we establish this fact to all orders
n\geqslant 0
.

6
Hierarchy uniqueness

Herein we prove that at each order
n\geqslant 0
, the Pöppe polynomial signature
expansion
\pi_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{e}_{n}([\boldsymbol{0}])=\pi_{n}
, exists, and is unique. This is our main result.
Before presenting this result in the general case, we present one further example, the
n=5
case.
This case acts a useful reference for our general argument.

Example 12
(Fifth order quintic modified Korteweg–de Vries equation:
n=5
)

In this case
\mathfrak{e}_{5}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{0}5\boldsymbol{0}]
and
the Pöppe polynomial
\pi_{5}
, in general, has the form,

\displaystyle\pi_{5}\coloneqq

\displaystyle\;c_{5}\cdot[\boldsymbol{5}]+c_{300}\cdot[\boldsymbol{3}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}+c_{030}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{3}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+c_{003}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}\,[\boldsymbol{3}]+c_{210}\cdot[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+c_{201}\cdot[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]+c_{120}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+c_{102}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{2}]+c_{021}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]

\displaystyle\;+c_{012}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{2}]+c_{111}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]+c_{10000}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{4}+c_{01000}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{3}

\displaystyle\;+c_{00100}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{2}+c_{00010}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]^{3}\,[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]+c_{00001}\cdot[\boldsymbol{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]^{4}.

The signature expansion for
[\boldsymbol{5}]
has the form,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{5}]=

\displaystyle\;\chi(5)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}5\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(41)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(32)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(23)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(14)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(311)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(221)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(212)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(131)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(122)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(113)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(2111)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(1211)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(1121)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1112)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(11111)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}].

Using the skew and symmetric Pöppe products in Lemma
8
we find, for example, that,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{1}]

\displaystyle\,[\boldsymbol{2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\chi(1)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)\,\bigl(\chi(2)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]+\chi(11)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)\,\bigl(\chi(0)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\chi(1)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)\,\bigl(\chi(2{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl\{\boldsymbol{0}3[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr\}+\chi(2\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\{\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}

\displaystyle\;\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\chi(11{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr\}+\chi(11\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\chi(1{{\otimes}}2{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}2\{\boldsymbol{0}3[\boldsymbol{0}]\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\{4[\boldsymbol{0}]\}\bigr]\bigr)+\chi(1\hat{{{\otimes}}}2{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}3[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1{{\otimes}}2\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}2\{\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\{3\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]\bigr)+\chi(1\hat{{{\otimes}}}2\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1{{\otimes}}11{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}2\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2[\boldsymbol{0}]\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\{1\boldsymbol{0}2[\boldsymbol{0}]\}\bigr]\bigr)+\chi(1\hat{{{\otimes}}}11{{\otimes}}0)\cdot\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}2[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr]

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1{{\otimes}}11\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\bigl(\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}2\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]+\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\{2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}\bigr]\bigr)

\displaystyle\;+\chi(1\hat{{{\otimes}}}11\hat{{{\otimes}}}0)\cdot\bigl[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\bigr].

The other products shown in
\pi_{5}
can be similarly expanded. In Tables
3
and
4

we list the essential basis elements and corresponding signature coefficients generated by all the Pöppe products present in
\pi_{5}
.
The values of the coefficients are the
\chi
-images of the tensored terms shown. Each row generates a linear algebraic
equation for the expansion coefficients
c_{5}
,
c_{300}
,
c_{210}
, …
c_{00001}
.
The ordering of rows and columns in Tables
3
and
4
is self-evident.
We discuss this ordering in detail presently.
Using all the rows shown, we generate an over-determined linear system of algebraic equations,
AC=B
,
where
B
is the column vector shown in the right-hand column in Table
4
and
C
is the vector of coefficients
c_{5}
,

c_{030}
,
c_{300}
,
c_{021}
and so forth in the order shown. The matrix
A
is populated with the
\chi
-images of the signature coefficients
shown in the tables. We can in fact solve
AC=B
for
C
systematically, block by block, as follows.
The first two rows corresponding to
[\boldsymbol{0}5\boldsymbol{0}]
and
\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}5\boldsymbol{0}
generate the pair of equations,

c_{5}+2c_{030}=1
and
c_{030}=0
. This system of equations corresponds to the smaller augmented subsystem
[A_{0}\,B_{0}^{\prime}]

where
A_{0}
is the same matrix as in Example
10
for the order
n=4
case, and
B_{0}^{\prime}=(1,0)^{\mathrm{T}}
.
We deduce
c_{5}=1
and
c_{030}=0
. The next two rows corresponding to
[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}1\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
generate
the pair of equations
5c_{5}+2c_{030}+2c_{300}+2c_{021}=0
and
2c_{030}-2c_{300}+2c_{021}=0
for
c_{300}
and
c_{021}
.
This pair corresponds to the smaller augmented subsystem
[A_{1}\,B_{1}^{\prime}]
where
A_{1}
is the same matrix as in Example
10
,
and
B_{1}^{\prime}=(-5,0)^{\mathrm{T}}
. Hence we deduce
c_{021}=-5/4
and
c_{300}=-5/4
. Then the equations corresponding to
the rows
[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}2\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

generate the following smaller augmented matrix subsystem
[A_{2}\,B_{2}^{\prime}]
for
c_{210}
,
c_{201}
,
c_{012}
and
c_{01000}
, where,

A_{2}
is the same as the corresponding matrix in Example
10
and
B_{2}^{\prime}=(-25/4,-5/4,5/4,5/4)^{\mathrm{T}}
.
This system of linear equations is easily solved to reveal
c_{210}=-5/4
,
c_{201}=-5/2
,
c_{012}=-5/4
and
c_{01000}=0
.
As we shall see, it is no coincidence that the coefficent matrices
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
match those for the
analogous blocks of basis elements in Example
10
.
The next set of rows corresponding to the basis elements

[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}3\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}3\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}3\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
generates exactly the same augmented matrix subsystem
[A_{2}\,B_{2}^{\prime}]
as that discussed just above,
but now for the unknown coefficients
c_{120}
,
c_{102}
,
c_{003}
and
c_{00010}
.
This linear system reveals
c_{120}=-5/4
,
c_{102}=-5/2
,
c_{003}=-5/4
and
c_{00010}=0
.
We do not deduce any new information from the equations corresponding to the rows
[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}4\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
other than that they are consistent.
We consider the next block of four rows shown in Tables
3
and
4
corresponding to
the rows
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}1\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}2\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}2\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}1\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]
.
These basis elements generate the following augmented matrix subsystem
[A_{3}^{\prime}\,B_{3}]
for
c_{111}
,
c_{10000}
,
c_{00100}
and
c_{00001}
, where,

A_{3}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&4&4&4\\
1&-4&-4&4\\
1&-4&4&-4\\
1&4&-4&-4\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}5\\
-5\\
-5\\
-5\end{pmatrix}.

This system of linear equations is easily solved to reveal
c_{111}=-5/2
,
c_{10000}=5/8
,
c_{00002}=5/8
and
c_{00001}=5/8
.
We have determined the unique set of coefficients for which
\mathfrak{e}_{5}\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)=\pi_{5}
.
In principle we can check the equations generated by the rows corresponding to the remaining basis elements are consistent.
However, we explain in our proof of our main result (Step 8) why this is not necessary.
Hence the fifth order non-commutative modified Korteweg–de Vries for
g=[\![G]\!]
, with
\mathcal{M}_{5}=\mu_{5}\,{\mathrm{id}}
,
is given by (this matches the form given in Nijhoff et al.
(
NQVDLCI
, eq. B.5a)
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{5}^{-1}{\partial}_{t}g=

\displaystyle\;{\partial}^{5}g-\frac{5}{4}\Bigl(\bigl({\partial}^{3}g\bigr)g^{2}+g^{2}\bigl({\partial}^{3}g\bigr)+\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)g

\displaystyle\;+2\,\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)g\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)+\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)g+2\,\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)g\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)

\displaystyle\;+g\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)+g\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}^{2}g\bigr)+2\,\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\;+\frac{5}{8}\Bigl(\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)g^{4}+g^{2}\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)g^{2}+g^{4}\bigl({\partial}g\bigr)\Bigr).

\qquad~\vdots

We now consider the general order
n\geqslant 0
case. Our goal is to establish the following.

Theorem 6.1
(Main result: existence and uniqueness)

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, there exists a unique Pöppe polynomial
\pi_{n}=\pi_{n}([\boldsymbol{0}],[\boldsymbol{1}],\ldots,[\boldsymbol{n}])

in
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
such that
\mathfrak{e}_{n}([\boldsymbol{0}])=\pi_{n}
.

We prove this result through a sequence of steps. It requires some preparation and the
rest of this section is devoted to outlining the notation, strategy, ideas
and intermediate results we require to carry through the proof in Steps 1–7 before giving the
overall proof in Step 8. We have the following immediate corollary of Theorem
6.1
.

Corollary 3
(The non-commutative Lax hierarchy is unique)

For any integer
n\geqslant 0
, the Lax hierarchy generated by the iteration indicated in
Corollary
1
is unique. The non-commutative nonlinear equation at order
n
in the Lax hierarchy
is simply that represented by
\mathfrak{e}_{n}([\boldsymbol{0}])=\pi_{n}
in Theorem
6.1
.

Proof

When
n
is even, we know from Corollary
1

that
\mathfrak{e}_{n+1}([\boldsymbol{0}])=({\partial}-A)\pi_{n}
. Now from Theorem
6.1
we know

\mathfrak{e}_{n+1}([\boldsymbol{0}])=\pi_{n+1}
with
\pi_{n+1}=\pi_{n+1}([\boldsymbol{0}],[\boldsymbol{1}],\ldots,[\boldsymbol{n}+1])

a unique polynomial in its arguments. We thus deduce the result of the corollary when
n
is even.
An exactly analogous argument follows for the case when
n
is odd.∎

Let us now return to the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
Roughly, in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we construct a ‘table’ for the coefficients of
\pi_{n}
,
much like we did for the cases
n=3,4,5
in Tables
1
—
4
.
Indeed, Tables
3
and
4
act as a useful reference.
We proceed systematically, considering basis elements
[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
in descending order with respect to the composition

\mathcal{C}
-component
w
and using a natural binary order for the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
-component
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
.
Both these orders are implicit in Tables
1
—
4
.

Step 1: Descent and binary order.

We introduce an order, the descent order, on the set of basis elements. We also define a new respresentation for the
basis elements, the composition-binary representation, that we use hereafter.
The descent order for compositions is given in Malham
Malham:KdVhierarchy
; we present it here for completeness.

Definition 15
(Descent ordering of compositions)

A composition
u\in{\mathcal{C}}
precedes another composition
v\in{\mathcal{C}}
if the length of the compostion
u
, i.e. the number of digits it contains,
is strictly less than the length of
v
. If
u
and
v
have the same length, say
k
, so
u=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{k}
and
v=v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k}
,
then
u
precedes
v
if for some
\ell\in\{1,2,\dots,k\}
we have
u_{1}=v_{1}
,
u_{2}=v_{2}
, …,
u_{\ell-1}=v_{\ell-1}
and
u_{\ell}<v_{\ell}
.
Otherwise,
v
precedes
u
. The resulting ordering induced on
{\mathcal{C}}
, is the
descent ordering
.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
-component
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in the basis element
[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
is any
(|w|+1)
-tuple constructed from

\{\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Recall from Remark
14
,
we can always arrange for the first component of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
to be ‘
\boldsymbol{0}
’; see Tables
1
—
4
.
Thus for any given composition
w\in\mathcal{C}
, the component
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
in a basis element
[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]

is one of the following forms,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0},~\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger},~\boldsymbol{\phi}_{3}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0},~\boldsymbol{\phi}_{4}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\phi}_{5}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0},~\boldsymbol{\phi}_{6}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger},~\boldsymbol{\phi}_{7}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0},

and so forth, all the way up to
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2^{|w|}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\cdots\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}
.
This is the
natural binary ordering
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
we refer to just above.
In this and the next sections, we mainly use a modified encoding of the basis elements
[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]
, as follows.
We replace the free monoid
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
of all forms
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
that can be constructed from
\{\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}

by the vector space
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
representing the span over all the elements
\mathbb{B}\coloneqq\{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1}
.
We thus express any
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
in the form
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=\beta_{1}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}+\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2}+\beta_{3}\boldsymbol{\phi}_{3}+\cdots
, where the
\beta_{i}

for integer
i\geqslant 1
, represent the coefficients of the basis element components
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{i}
. Henceforth we
represent any element
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}
by a
2^{|w|}
-tuple
\boldsymbol{\beta}\coloneqq(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{2^{|w|}})\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
.
Thus we replace,

[w\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]\rightsquigarrow[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}.

Example 13

Some examples matching the old notation with the new are as follows:
[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}]=[a]\times(1,0)
,
[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]=[a]\times(0,1)
, and then also,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(1,0,0,0),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,1,0,0),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,0,1,0),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,0,0,1),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}a_{3}\boldsymbol{0}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}]\times(1,0,0,0,0,0,0,0),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}a_{3}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}]\times(0,1,0,0,0,0,0,0),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}a_{3}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}]\times(0,0,0,0,0,0,0,1),

and so forth. Naturally, as constructed, for linear combinations, we have for example that for any real scalar constants
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
,

\beta_{1}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}]+\beta_{2}\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}a_{1}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}a_{2}\boldsymbol{0}]=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(\beta_{1},0,\beta_{2},0),

and so forth. There is a natural basis for elements of
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
of a given length
2^{n}
. Such a basis is
given by the elements of length
2^{n}
of the form
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}\coloneqq(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots)
, where the ‘
1
’ is in the
i
th position.

Definition 16
(Composition-binary representation)

We call the representation
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
where
w\in\mathcal{C}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}

the
composition-binary representation
of the basis elements. We call
[w]
the composition component and

\boldsymbol{\beta}
the
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
-component.

Step 2: Triple product action.

All the Pöppe polynomials
\pi_{n}
are polynomials in the skew-Pöppe algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]

and thus necessarily of odd degree. As such all the monomials therein can be constructed from triple products
of signature expansions
[\boldsymbol{n}]
, as we have seen in Examples
8
—
12
.
In particular we characterise the following triple product action which is established straightforwardly using
the Pöppe product rules in Lemma
8
.

Lemma 12

Consider the two signature expansions
[\boldsymbol{a}]
and
[\boldsymbol{b}]
and a basis element
[cw\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]\in\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
,
with
c\in\mathbb{Z}
the first letter in the composition ‘
cw
’. Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
denote the element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\ast}

given by
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
with its first letter ‘
\,\boldsymbol{0}
’ removed.
Then at leading order the triple product action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]\bigr)
is given by,

\displaystyle\chi(a{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)\cdot\Bigl(

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]+[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}b\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}b\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}b\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]+[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}b\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+2)\boldsymbol{0}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+2\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+2)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}c(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]+[\boldsymbol{0}a\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]\Bigr)+\cdots.

Here by leading order, we mean, we do not retain terms generated by lower (descent) order terms
in the signature expansions of
[\boldsymbol{a}]
and
[\boldsymbol{b}]
, nor do we retain terms generated with a quasi-product term—i.e.
generated using any of the final terms with real factor ‘
\,2
’ in the Pöppe products in Lemma
8
.
We use the notation ‘
+\cdots
’ do denote these missing terms.

We also define the following auto-tensorial action on
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
.

Definition 17
(Tensorial action)

Given
\boldsymbol{\beta}\coloneqq(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots)
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\coloneqq(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots)

in
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
we define the (left)
auto-tensorial action
of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
on
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
,
denoted
\boldsymbol{\beta}\lhd\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, to be,

\boldsymbol{\beta}\lhd\boldsymbol{\gamma}\coloneqq\bigl(\beta_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},\beta_{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma},\ldots\bigr),

where for each
i=1,2,\ldots
, we note
\beta_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(\beta_{i}\gamma_{1},\beta_{i}\gamma_{2},\ldots)
.

In the new notation, with the tensorial action on
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
just defined,
the triple product action on
\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
given in Lemma
12

can be expressed more succinctly as follows.

Corollary 4
(Triple product action)

Given two signature expansions
[\boldsymbol{a}]
and
[\boldsymbol{b}]
and a generic basis element

[cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}]\times\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{Z}_{\boldsymbol{0}}]
,
the triple product action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)
on
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{C}]\times\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}

is given at leading order by,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)\phantom{uuuu}

\displaystyle=\chi(a{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)\cdot\Bigl(

\displaystyle[(a+1)(b+1)cw]\times\bigl((1,0,1,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\,(0,1,0,1)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[(a+1)b(c+1)w]\times\bigl((1,0,0,1)\lhd(\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger})\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[a(b+2)cw]\times\bigl((2,0,0,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\,(0,2,0,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle[a(b+1)(c+1)w]\times\bigl((1,1,0,0)\lhd(\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger})\bigr)\Bigr)+\cdots.

Here, if
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\ldots,\beta_{2^{n}})
, then
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}=(\beta_{2^{n}},\beta_{2^{n}-1},\ldots,\beta_{2},\beta_{1})
.

Proof

The result of the corollary is just a restatement of the triple product action in Lemma
12
.
That the adjoint of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, denoted
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}
, corresponds to reversing the elements in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is explained as follows.
The entries in
\boldsymbol{\beta}
correspond to the coefficients of the basis
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}
,
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2}
,
\ldots
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2^{n}}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The triple product action in Lemma
12
involves the components
\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}^{\dagger}
where

\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
with its first letter ‘
\,\boldsymbol{0}
’ removed. Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{1}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2}
,
\ldots
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2^{n}}
,
be the same sequence of basis elements each of which has the first letter ‘
\,\boldsymbol{0}
’ removed. We observe,

\bigl\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{1}^{\dagger},\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2}^{\dagger},\ldots,\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2^{n}}^{\dagger}\bigr\}=\bigl\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2^{n}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2^{n}-1},\ldots,\hat{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{1}\bigr\}
.
∎

Example 14

Consider computing the triple product
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,[\boldsymbol{c}]
to leading order. In this case,
to leading order
[\boldsymbol{c}]=[\boldsymbol{0}c\boldsymbol{0}]+\cdots
and so in Corollary
4
we have
w=\nu
, the empty word, with
|w|=0
.
We observe,
[\boldsymbol{0}c\boldsymbol{0}]=[c]\times\boldsymbol{\gamma}
with
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=(1,0)\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
.
Hence we have,

\displaystyle(1,0,1,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigl(1\cdot(1,0),\,0\cdot(1,0),\,1\cdot(1,0),\,0\cdot(1,0)\,\bigr)=(1,0,0,0,1,0,0,0),

\displaystyle(0,1,0,1)\lhd\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\bigl(0\cdot(0,1),\,1\cdot(0,1),\,0\cdot(0,1),\,1\cdot(0,1)\,\bigr)=(0,0,0,1,0,0,0,1),

and so forth. Hence we observe from Corollary
4
that at leading order,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,[\boldsymbol{c}]=\chi(a{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}c)\cdot\bigl(

\displaystyle[(a+1)(b+1)c]\times(1,0,0,1,1,0,0,1)

\displaystyle\;+[(a+1)b(c+1)]\times(1,1,0,0,0,0,1,1)

\displaystyle\;+[a(b+2)c]\times(2,0,0,2,0,0,0,0)

\displaystyle\;+[a(b+1)(c+1)]\times(1,1,1,1,0,0,0,0)\bigr)+\cdots.

We extensively use such computations hereafter.

Step 3: Generators, a coarse-grain overview.

For a given basis element
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
and composition
w
of
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it is
useful to identify the types of odd-degree monomials of signature expansions that might generate it.

Definition 18
(Monomial generator)

Given a basis element
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
with a composition component
[w]
and an
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
-component
\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
where
w
is a composition of
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
has length
2^{n}
, we call any odd-degree
monomial of signature expansions of the form
[\boldsymbol{a}_{1}]\,[\boldsymbol{a}_{2}]\,\cdots\,[\boldsymbol{a}_{2m+1}]
that produces

[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
as one of the terms in its expansion, a
monomial generator
or just
generator
of
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

At this stage and in this step, it is useful to give a brief coarse overview of our overall strategy,
which we implement in detail in the subsequent steps below. We show in this step how, for any given composition component,
we can identify, for the associated basis elements, specific collections of generators. We call the sets of
basis elements and corresponding collections of generators “coefficient blocks” or simply “blocks”.
We show that such blocks are necessarily square.
To start, consider any one-part composition
w
of
n
, so the two corresponding basis elements
are
[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}]\rightsquigarrow[n]\times(1,0)
and
[\boldsymbol{0}n\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}]\rightsquigarrow[n]\times(0,1)
.
The basis element
[n]\times(1,0)
is generated by signature expansion
[\boldsymbol{n}]
, while

[n]\times(0,1)
is not. On the other hand, both basis elements are generated by
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{n-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
.
This exhausts all the possible odd-degree monomials in
\pi_{n}
that could generate

[n]\times(1,0)
and
[n]\times(0,1)
. Thus for a one-part composition, the possible odd-degree generators
have the form,

[\star],~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}]),

where
[\star]
represents the appropriate generic signature expansion, i.e. in the first instance it is
[\boldsymbol{n}]

and in the second instance, i.e. for
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
, the middle
[\star]
factor is
[\boldsymbol{n-2}]
.
Note, we allow
[\star]=[\boldsymbol{0}]
.
The only other possibilities are
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]
.
However for
n\geqslant 3
, we can rule these two possibilities out as
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]=2\cdot\{\boldsymbol{0}1\boldsymbol{0}\}
and any subsequent Pöppe product
of this term with
[\boldsymbol{0}]
would generate a basis element
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
where the composition
w
has two parts.
Note of course, the triple Pöppe product
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,[\boldsymbol{c}]
is naturally associative.

Now consider any two part composition
w=a_{1}a_{2}
of
n
. We observe that basis elements with such a two-part
composition component can in principle be generated by
[\star]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
, which we have already come across just above.
However such basis elements can also be generated by any of the following four generators of the form,

[\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}]),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star]),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\star]),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr).

We observe that each possible generator above contains only two ‘
[\star]
’ factors, consistent with the
two-part composition component of the basis elements we are aiming to generate. Further note that
we can also see that the four generators above can be constructed from the previous two generators

[\star]
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
corresponding to one-part compositions, by applying
one of the three actions
[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
,
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
or
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to them.
For example, the first generator above is constucted by applying the action
[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to
[\star]=[\boldsymbol{0}]
,
where we must set the argument
[\star]=[\boldsymbol{0}]
to preserve the two-part composition component of the basis element
we wish to generate. The next two generators above are constructed by applying the actions

[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
or
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to
[\star]
. Now consider the final quintic generator above.
Applying the action
[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
would produce a generator with too many ‘
[\star]
’
factors, while in principle, either of the actions
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
or
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
could be
applied to
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
. However the action of
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
on
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)

is nilpotent. We demonstrate this below in Lemma
16
. Hence the action

[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
rigorously applied to
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
produces zero. Thus the
only viable action is
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
on
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])
producing the quintic generator shown.
From another perspective, for the quintic generator, in the case
n\geqslant 5
, any other quintic arrangment with
two
[\star]
- and three
[\boldsymbol{0}]
-factors, would necessitate a consecutive pair ‘
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’ that would result
in generating a basis element whose composition component has more than two parts.

In the case of basis elements with a three-part composition component
w=a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
,
the possible generators are, in principle, any of the generators we have already seen, as well as,
the generators of the form,

\displaystyle[\star]\,[\star]\,([\star]),~

\displaystyle[\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\star])\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr).

We remark that each possible generator above contains only three ‘
[\star]
’ factors.
We see that the first two generators are constructed by applying the action
[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)

to the generators for basis elements with one-part composition components.
The next set of generators are constructed by applying the action
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
to
the generators for basis elements with two-part composition components, taking into account
the nilpotent action of
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
on
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
.
This accounts for the next two generators. Then the final four generators are constructed
by applying the action
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to all four of the generators
for basis elements with two-part composition components.

For the case of basis elements with a four-part composition component
w=a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}a_{4}
,
the possible generators are, in principle, besides any of the generators we have already seen,
generators of the form,

\displaystyle[\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\star])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\star])\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\star])\bigr),~[\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),~[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr)\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr)\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,([\star])\bigr)\bigr),~[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\star])\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,([\boldsymbol{0}])\bigr)\bigr)\bigr).

Again, each possible generator above contains only four ‘
[\star]
’ factors.
They are constructed by applying the action

[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to the generators for basis elements with two-part composition components,
applying the action
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
to
the generators for basis elements with three-part composition components, taking into account
the nilpotent action of
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
on
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
, and
then also applying the action
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to all of the generators
for basis elements with three-part composition components.

We have seen that for basis elements with a composition component with
k=1,2,3
or
4
parts,
the number of generators that might produce such a basis element is
2^{k}
.
We have not shown that corresponding to a given basis element, the generators
constructed in the manner indicated are unique at leading order. We demonstrate
this below in Steps
7
and
8
. Assuming this is the case for the moment,
we have the following.

Lemma 13
(Generator block size)

For a given basis element with composition component
w
, the number of
monomial generators that can generate that basis element at leading order is
2^{|w|}
.

Proof

As observed, the result is true for
|w|=1,2,3,4
. Assume the result is
true for
|w|=1,2,\ldots,k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The set of generators for basis elements with composition components of
k+1
parts
are constructed by: (i) Applying the action
[\star]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)

to the generators for basis elements with
(k-1)
-part composition components of which there are
2^{k-1}
by assumption;
(ii) Applying the action
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
to the generators for basis elements with
k
-part composition components, taking into account
the nilpotent action of
[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
on
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
. Since there are
2^{k}
generators corresponding to any
basis element with a composition of
k
-parts, and half of these start with the factor ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\,[\star]
’, there are
2^{k-1}

generators constructed in this way; and then finally (iii) Applying the action
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,(\cdot)
to all of the generators
for basis elements with
k
-part composition components, of which there are
2^{k}
. Adding these three contributions up,
2^{k-1}+2^{k-1}+2^{k}=2^{k+1}
,
and the result follows by induction.
∎

We can also view this last result from another perspective as follows.
For each
k
-part composition, when
k
is odd, the set of new generators are characterised
as follows. First, we include the degree
k
monomial
[\star]\,[\star]\,\cdots\,[\star]
,
of which there is only one choice. We also include the degree
k+2
monomials which
contain two non-adjacent ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’ factors; there are
k+1
choose
2
possible monomials of this form.
Then we can also include degree
k+4
monomials which contain four non-adjacent ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’ factors;
there are
k+1
choose
4
possible monomials of this form. And so forth until we reach the single
degree
2k+1
monomial of the form
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\cdots\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
.
Here we have implicitly used that the number of ways to place
r
objects in non-adjacent slots whose
total number is
m
, is given by
m-r+1
choose
r
. In the examples just presented, we
considered the number of possible ways of placing
2\ell
factors of the form ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’ in
a monomial of degree
k+2\ell
, for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,(k+1)/2
. Hence the total number of
monomials of each degree outlined being
k+1
choose
2\ell
. Thus with
k
odd, the total number of
such new odd-degree monomials is given by the sum over
\ell=0,1,\ldots,(k+1)/2
of
k+1
choose
2\ell
,
i.e. the sum on the left shown in Lemma
14
.
Suppose now
k
is even. The lowest degree monomials that might generate the corresponding basis element
are those of degree
k+1
with a single factor ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’. There are
k+1
such monomials.
We can also include degree
k+3
monomials with three non-adjacent factors ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’; there
are
k+1
choose
3
such possible monomials, and so forth. In the final highest degree monomial,
of degree
2k+1
has the single form
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\cdots\,[\star]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
.
Thus with
k
even, the total number of such new odd-degree monomials is given by the sum
over
\ell=0,1,\ldots,k/2
of
k+1
choose
2\ell+1
, i.e. the sum on the right shown in Lemma
14
.
In consequence we have the following important result.

Lemma 14

The aforementioned sums, in the respective
k
is odd and then even cases, are equal to
2^{k}
.
In other words, respectively, when
k
is odd and then even, we have,

\sum_{\ell=0}^{(k+1)/2}\begin{pmatrix}k+1\\
2\ell\end{pmatrix}=2^{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{\ell=0}^{k/2}\begin{pmatrix}k+1\\
2\ell+1\end{pmatrix}=2^{k}.

Proof

Suppose
k
is odd. Then by direct computation, we observe,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{(k+1)/2}\!\frac{(k+1)!}{(k+1-2\ell)!(2\ell)!}=

\displaystyle\;2+\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{(k-1)/2}\!\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell)!(2\ell-1)!}\biggl(\frac{1}{k-2\ell+1}+\frac{1}{2\ell}\biggr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;2+\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{(k-1)/2}\!\frac{k!}{(k+1-2\ell)!(2\ell-1)!}+\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{(k-1)/2}\!\frac{k!}{(k-2\ell)!(2\ell)!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
2\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
3\end{pmatrix}+\cdots+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
k-1\end{pmatrix}+1,

where we matched up respective pairs from the sums and then used that
2^{k}=(1+1)^{k}
. This gives the first result.
When
k
is even, we again use that
2^{k}=(1+1)^{k}
, and observe,

2^{k}=\begin{pmatrix}k\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
1\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
2\end{pmatrix}+\cdots+\begin{pmatrix}k\\
k\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k/2}\frac{(k+1)!}{(k-2\ell)!(2\ell+1)!},

where we paired up successive terms and parameterised the pairs by
\ell=0,1,\ldots,k/2
.
This gives the second result.
∎

The crucial observation from the result of Lemmas
13
and
14
is the following.

Corollary 5
(Generator-tuple dimension match)

For a given composition component
w
of a block set of basis elements
[w]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
parameterised
by the tuples
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
,
the number of new monomial generators equals the dimension of the tuple block, i.e.
2^{|w|}
.

One of our main concerns now is to show that the resulting square block of signature coefficients has full rank.
The next three steps address this issue, making the analysis of this section more precise.

Step 4: The three standard triple actions.

We have seen that the triple product action in Corollary
4
, in the full form given therein,
as well as in the special forms
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
,
are used to construct the generators corresponding to a given basis element. We call these three actions the
standard triple actions.

Definition 19
(Standard triple actions)

We call the actions
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
,
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)

and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
the three
standard
triple actions.

The result of the action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
is given in Corollary
4
.
As we use them frequently hereafter, we record the result of the standard actions

[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)
in the following Corollary.
They are just special cases which we call the
special actions
.

Corollary 6
(Special actions)

The two special actions
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)
and
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl(\cdot\bigr)

are given at leading order by,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\;\chi(a{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}cw)\!\cdot\![(a+1)(c+1)w]\times\bigl((1,-1)\lhd\bigl(\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)\bigr)+\cdots,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\;\chi(0{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)\!\cdot\!\Bigl([(b+2)cw]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}(0,2)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)

\displaystyle\;\qquad\qquad+[(b+1)(c+1)w]\times\bigl((1,1)\lhd\bigl(\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)+\cdots.

Proof

By direct computation using the Pöppe product rules in Lemma
8
, we observe that

[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([cw\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]\bigr)
equals,

\displaystyle\chi(a{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}cw)\cdot\bigl(

\displaystyle\;[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle\;-[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]-[\boldsymbol{0}(a+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}\bigr)+\cdots,

at leading order, giving the first result.
Then, by direct computation for the other case, we observe that
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw\times\boldsymbol{\varphi}]\bigr)
equals,

\displaystyle\chi(0{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)\cdot\bigl(

\displaystyle\;2\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}(b+2)\boldsymbol{0}c)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+2\cdot[\boldsymbol{0}(b+2)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}c)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle\;+[\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]+[\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})]

\displaystyle\;+[\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}]+[\boldsymbol{0}(b+1)\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}(c+1)(w\times\hat{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})^{\dagger}\bigr)+\cdots,

at leading order, giving the second result. ∎

Remark 15

Comparing the results of Corollary
6
with Corollary
4
we emphasise
two observations, that there is: (i) A natural contraction of the action forms due to the ‘
\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
’ factors in the action;
(ii) An apparent change of sign in the second term on the right in the first example. We can view both cases as
the consequence of substituting
[\nu\times\boldsymbol{0}]
for
[\boldsymbol{b}]
in the first case and then
[\nu\times\boldsymbol{0}]
for
[\boldsymbol{a}]

in the second case. The sign change, perhaps more easily observed from the corresponding result in Lemma
12
,
is a consquence of the fact that to make the appropriate substitution of
[\nu\times\boldsymbol{0}]
for
[\boldsymbol{b}]
, we should
convert the two terms with
\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}b\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}
on the right, to
-\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}b^{\dagger}\boldsymbol{0}^{\dagger}
first.

Step 5: Generating blocks.
We now show precisely how, given a block of basis elements characterised
by a given composition component
w
and parameterised by the corresponding
2^{|w|}
basis elements
\boldsymbol{\beta}
of
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
,
we can use the three standard actions to enumerate all the monomial generators that produce the basis elements
of that block at leading order, and also establish the corresponding signature coefficient associated with
each such basis element. Let us examine the three standard actions given in
Corollaries
4
and
6
more closely.
If we examine the right-hand side of
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)

in Corollary
4
, then we observe that in terms of descent order, the first
composition term ‘
[(a+1)(b+1)cw]
’ on the right is highest, and thus we retain that term only.
In Corollary
6
, at leading order, the action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)
in unique, while
the right-hand side of
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,\bigl([cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)
contains two terms, the
first of which is higher in terms of descent order, which is the one we retain.
Thus at leading order the three standard actions on
[cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
are:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi(cw)\!\cdot\![(a+1)(b+1)cw]\!\times\!\bigl((1,0,1,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}\,(0,1,0,1)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)+\cdots,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi(cw)\!\cdot\![(a+1)(c+1)w]\!\times\!\bigl((1,-1)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}(1,-1)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)+\cdots,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi(cw)\!\cdot\![(b+2)cw]\!\times\!\bigl((2,0)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}+(-1)^{|w|}(0,2)\lhd\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\dagger}\bigr)+\cdots.

Here we have used the homomorphic properties of
\chi
, in particular that
\chi(a{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)=\chi(a{{\otimes}}0{{\otimes}}cw)=\chi(0{{\otimes}}b{{\otimes}}cw)=\chi(cw)
.
Consider the following respective replacements in each of the three actions above:
(i)
a\to a-1
,
b\to b-1
,
c\to\nu
; (ii)
a\to a-1
,
c\to c-1
and (iii)
b\to b-2
.
With these three choices, each of the actions generates the same composition
acw
—in the first case we relabel
b
as
c
and
in the third case we relabel
b
as
a
. Recall from our coarse-grain overview in Step 3 that to enumerate the generators
corresponding to basis elements with composition components with
k\geqslant 2
parts, we apply the first action to the generators
at level
k-2
, and the two special actions to the generators at level
k-1
, taking into account the nilpotent action outlined
just below in Lemma
16
.
We note that, for any sequence
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
, with
|\hat{u}|=2^{k-2}
, we have,

\displaystyle(1,0,1,0)\lhd\hat{u}

\displaystyle=(1,1)\lhd(1,0)\lhd\hat{u}=(1,1)\lhd(\hat{u},0),

\displaystyle(0,1,0,1)\lhd\hat{u}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(1,1)\lhd(0,1)\lhd\hat{u}^{\dagger}=(1,1)\lhd(0,{\hat{u}}^{\dagger}),

where
(\hat{u},0)
and
(0,\hat{u}^{\dagger})
are of length
2^{k-1}
.
Putting these observations together, we have thus established the following lemma.

Lemma 15
(Actions generating the same composition)

At leading order, with the choices mentioned above, the following three standard actions generate the same composition with the
respective
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
components indicated,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{c-1}]\,\bigl([w]\!\times\!\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi(w)\cdot[acw]\times\bigl((1,1)\lhd\bigl((\hat{u},0)-(-1)^{|w|}(\hat{u},0)^{\dagger}\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{a-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([(c-1)w]\!\times\!\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi((c-1)w)\cdot[acw]\times\bigl((1,-1)\lhd\bigl((\hat{a},\hat{b})+(-1)^{|w|}(\hat{a},\hat{b})^{\dagger}\bigr)\bigr),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,\bigl([cw]\!\times\!\boldsymbol{\beta}\bigr)

\displaystyle\!=\!\chi(cw)\cdot[acw]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd(\hat{a},\hat{b})+(-1)^{|w|}(0,2)\lhd(\hat{a},\hat{b})^{\dagger}\bigr).

Here, in the first case
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\hat{u},0)\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
with
\hat{u}
arbitrary, and in the second and third cases

\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\hat{a},\hat{b})\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
is arbitrary. Each such
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is of length
2^{|acw|-1}
,
and
\hat{a}
and
\hat{b}
have the same length—matching that of
\hat{u}
.

Remark 16

Note, in the statement of Lemma
15
, the case of the first action which corresponds to
the action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)
applied to
[cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}
in the discussion preceding the Lemma.
In that discussion, when we set
c\to\nu
, we equivalently replaced
cw
by
w
. This means that
we should effectively consider the length of
w
to be one less than would otherwise be the
case. This explains why the sign in front of the term with the factor
(-1)^{|w|}
in the first action case is negative
in the statement of the Lemma.

Some further clarifications on the statement of Lemma
15
are required. Note that,

[\boldsymbol{0}]\rightsquigarrow[\nu]\times(1),

where
\nu
is the empty composition and
(1)
is the element of
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
corresponding to
compositions of zero parts. The special action
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,(\cdot)
in Lemma
15

still applies when the argument
[cw]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}=[\nu]\times(1)
and thus when
(\hat{a},\hat{b})=(1)
.
The result is that at leading order we have,

[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,\bigl([\nu]\times(1)\bigr)=\chi(\nu)\cdot[a]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd(1)-(0,2)\lhd(1)^{\dagger}\bigr)=[a]\times(2,-2).

Here, by convention, we take
\chi(\nu)\coloneqq 1
. Since we have taken
cw\to\nu
,
we can think of the number of parts of
w
to be ‘
-1
’, explaining the sign
in front of the
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
-element
(0,2)
. This is consistent with
just computing
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
. Further, the first two actions in Lemma
15

don’t make sense when
cw\to\nu
, though if
w\to\nu
, the special action
[\boldsymbol{a-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)

applies with the appropriate adaptations. And of course we can compute

[\boldsymbol{a-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{c-1}]\,\bigl([\nu]\times(1)\bigr)=[\boldsymbol{a-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{c-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
.

Finally, we now also observe the following (aforementioned) nilpotency property.

Lemma 16
(Nilpotent action)

At leading order, if we first apply the action
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)
to an arbitrary
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
component,
then apply the action
[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,(\cdot)
to the result, this generates the zero
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
component.
In other words at leading order we have,

[\boldsymbol{a}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{b}]\,(\cdot)\bigr)=0,

where the ‘
\,0
’ on the right-hand side represents the zero
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
component.

Proof

We focus on the effect of the actions on the
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
components only.
The third (special) action applied to the input
(a,b)
generates
2\cdot(a,b,\pm b^{\dagger},\pm a^{\dagger})
.
Set
A,B\in\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
to be the sub-components
A\coloneqq(a,b)
and
B\coloneqq\pm(b^{\dagger},a^{\dagger})
.
With these identifications we note that

B=\pm A^{\dagger}
. Ignoring the real factor
2
, apply the second (special) action to the input
(A,B)
.
This is (note the sign of the second term of the action changes),
(1,-1)\lhd\bigl((A,B)\mp(A,B)^{\dagger}\bigr)
,
which equals,
(A\mp B^{\dagger},B\mp A^{\dagger},-A\pm B^{\dagger},-B\pm A^{\dagger})
.
Since
B=\pm A^{\dagger}
, this result is the zero
\mathbb{R}{\langle}\mathbb{B}{\rangle}
component.
∎

We now explore, through a series of examples, how to construct the generators and coefficient blocks
associated with any given composition. In particular we consider the cases of compositions with

1
,
2
and
3
parts, before exploring the case of any given composition. Compositions
containing a ‘
1
’ need to be singled out, as explained below.

Example 15
(One-part compositions)

We observe that there are two basis elements corresponding to the one-part composition
w=a
, namely,

[a]\times(1,0)
and
[a]\times(0,1)
. We assume
n=a\geqslant 2
. At leading we know from the corresponding
signature expansion
[\boldsymbol{a}]=[a]\times(1,0)+\cdots
. From our discussion succeeding Lemma
15
,
we know the first two actions do not make sense when
cw\to\nu
, while the final special action
does make sense. As we saw directly, at leading order we have
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]=[a]\times(2,-2)
.
We have thus enumerated the generators corresponding to
[a]\times(1,0)
and
[a]\times(0,1)
and that
the signature coefficient matrix is,

A_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\
0&-2\end{pmatrix}.

Example 16
(Two-part compositions)

Consider the basis elements with a two-part composition component
a_{1}a_{2}
, i.e. basis
elements of the form
[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}
, where the
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}
are the
four basis elements of length
4
, which are zero apart from a ‘
1
’ in the
i
th position.
For the moment assume neither
a_{1}
nor
a_{2}
equal
1
; we consider each of these two special
cases separately below. Using Lemma
15
, noting that for
each of the standard actions our goal is to obtain the composition component
[a_{1}a_{2}]

on the right-hand side, we observe the following. For the first action, setting
w=\nu
,

a=a_{1}
and
c=a_{2}
, we find that at leading order, we get,

[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\bigl((1,1)\lhd\bigl((1,0)-(0,1)\bigr)\bigr)=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(1,-1,1,-1).

The first special action in Lemma
15
, with the same identifications gives,

[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([a_{2}-1]\times(\hat{a},\hat{b})\bigr)=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\bigl((1,-1)\lhd\bigl((\hat{a},\hat{b})+(\hat{b},\hat{a})\bigr)\bigr).

We saw in Example
15
, the basis element
[a_{2}-1]\times(\hat{a},\hat{b})

can be generated both by the corresponding signature expansion
[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]=[a_{2}-1]\times(1,0)+\cdots
,
and by the generator
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
. We discount the latter case due to the nilpotent action property.
Hence using this expression for
[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]
and inserting
(\hat{a},\hat{b})=(0,1)
into the expression above, we deduce,

[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]\bigr)=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(1,1,-1,-1),

to leading order. Now consider the second special action in Lemma
15
.
Again with the same identifications for
a
,
c
and
w
, we observe that to leading order,

[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-2}]\,\bigl([a_{2}]\times(\hat{a},\hat{b})\bigr)=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd(\hat{a},\hat{b})+(0,2)\lhd(\hat{b},\hat{a})\bigr).

We know from Example
15
, the basis element
[a_{2}]\times(\hat{a},\hat{b})

can be generated either by the signature expansion
[\boldsymbol{a_{2}}]=[a_{2}]\times(1,0)+\cdots
,
or by the generator
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]=[a_{2}]\times(2,-2)+\cdots
. Respectively substituting the expressions

[a_{2}]\times(1,0)
and
[a_{2}]\times(2,-2)
for
[a_{2}]\times(\hat{a},\hat{b})
in the relation just above, we observe that to leading order,

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-2}]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{a_{2}}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd(1,0)+(0,2)\lhd(0,1)\bigr)

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(2,0,0,2),

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-2}]\,\bigl([\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times\bigl((2,0)\lhd(2,-2)+(0,2)\lhd(-2,2)\bigr)

\displaystyle=[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(4,-4,-4,4).

We have thus enumerated the four generators corresponding to the four basis elements
[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(1,0,0,0)
,

[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,1,0,0)
,
[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,0,1,0)
and
[a_{1}a_{2}]\times(0,0,0,1)
. They are

[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]
,
[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-1}]\,[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}-1}]
,
[\boldsymbol{0}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{1}-2}]\,[\boldsymbol{a_{2}}]

and the quintic generator
